# Content audit 2026-09 — open findings
Multi-agent content review of the 16 guides, their six translations, the quiz/wizard
content in `src/i18n/locales/*.json`, the glossary, and every external URL the English
guides recommend. 51 review agents, then one adversarial verifier per finding.
**Read the caveat before trusting a row.** The verifier confirmed 395 of 399 findings and refuted 4.
A 99% confirm rate is not independence: reviewer and verifier are the same model. Every
`critical` row below was additionally checked by hand against the file. `high` and `medium`
rows carry the verifier's reasoning only. Re-open the file before acting on one.
| Severity | Count |
|---|---|
| critical | 25 |
| high | 92 |
| medium | 255 |
| low (verified) | 23 |
| low (unverified) | 341 |

## Findings

### critical
**1. [src/content/guides/ar/nip05-identity.mdx:310] Stale troubleshooting bullet tells the reader to verify their npub inside nostr.json, endorsing the exact misconfiguration that breaks NIP-05 verification.**

- Evidence: Under **"NIP-05 لا يعمل"** (line 310): "- تحقق مرة أخرى من أن npub الخاص بك صحيح في nostr.json". The EN source was rewritten (en lines 310-313) to say the opposite: "**Check the key format first.** `nostr.json` must contain your public key in lowercase hex — 64 characters, no `npub1` prefix. An `npub` here is the single most common cause of a NIP-05 that never verifies." A reader whose nostr.json contains an npub (the most common breakage) follows this bullet, confirms the npub is 'correct', and never finds the real cause. It also contradicts the correctly-translated hex warning callout in the same ar file (lines 239-248).
- Fix: Replace line 310 with a translation of the current EN bullet, e.g.: "- **تحقّق من صيغة المفتاح أولًا.** يجب أن يحتوي `nostr.json` على مفتاحك العام بصيغة hex بأحرف صغيرة — 64 حرفًا، دون بادئة `npub1`. وجود `npub` هنا هو السبب الأكثر شيوعًا لعدم نجاح التحقق من NIP-05 أبدًا." Note for the orchestrator: pl/nip05-identity.mdx line 311 carries the same stale pre-rewrite line.
**2. [src/content/guides/de/nip05-identity.mdx:312] The troubleshooting bullet still tells readers to verify their npub inside nostr.json, teaching the exact mistake (npub instead of lowercase hex) that produces a silently broken NIP-05; EN rewrote this bullet to say the opposite.**

- Evidence: Line 312, under '**"NIP-05 funktioniert nicht"**': "- Überprüfe, ob dein npub in nostr.json korrekt ist". EN source replaced this with: "**Check the key format first.** `nostr.json` must contain your public key in lowercase hex — 64 characters, no `npub1` prefix. An `npub` here is the single most common cause of a NIP-05 that never verifies." The DE bullet directly contradicts the (correctly translated) hex-key warning callout at line 240 of the same file.
- Fix: Replace the bullet with a translation of the current EN text: "- **Prüfe zuerst das Schlüsselformat.** `nostr.json` muss deinen öffentlichen Schlüssel als Kleinbuchstaben-Hex enthalten — 64 Zeichen, ohne `npub1`-Präfix. Ein `npub` an dieser Stelle ist die häufigste Ursache für ein NIP-05, das nie verifiziert wird."
**3. [src/content/guides/de/nip17-private-messages.mdx:123] The 'Hauptvorteile' list is a stale pre-correction version that makes two false privacy promises: it claims the recipient is hidden and that NIP-17 provides forward secrecy, contradicting the EN source, the NIP-17 spec, and the guide's own warning callout and comparison table.**

- Evidence: Lines 123-125: "1. **Metadaten-Privatsphäre**: Niemand außer dem Empfänger kann bestimmen, wer die Nachricht gesendet hat oder an wen sie gesendet wurde" and "3. **Bessere Forward Secrecy**: Jede Nachricht nutzt einzigartige ephemere Schlüssel". EN source (Key Benefits) says the opposite: "The recipient, however, is named in a plaintext `p` tag that relays must be able to read" and "**No forward secrecy**: NIP-44 derives its conversation key from two static keys, so an attacker who later obtains your `nsec` can decrypt every message they saved". The DE file's own callout at line 39 ("Relays sehen weiterhin, wem du schreibst") and table line 62 ("**Weiterhin sichtbar** — Klartext-`p`-Tag") contradict bullet 1.
- Fix: Replace the three bullets under '### Hauptvorteile' with a translation of the current EN list: "1. **Absender-Privatsphäre**: Niemand außer dem Empfänger kann bestimmen, wer die Nachricht gesendet hat. Der Empfänger steht allerdings in einem Klartext-`p`-Tag, den Relays lesen können müssen. 2. **Unverknüpfbarkeit**: Jede Nachricht ist mit einem frischen Einmal-Schlüssel signiert, sodass Nachrichten desselben Absenders nicht offensichtlich zusammengehören. 3. **Keine Forward Secrecy**: NIP-44 leitet den Konversationsschlüssel aus zwei statischen Schlüsseln ab. Wer später an deinen `nsec` kommt, kann jede gespeicherte Nachricht entschlüsseln. Die Einmal-Schlüssel verbergen, *wer gesendet hat*; alte Nachrichten schützen sie nicht. Lösche alte Konversationen, wenn dir das wichtig ist."
**4. [src/content/guides/de/relays-demystified.mdx:174] The NIP-65 explanation inverts read/write relay semantics, teaching a false protocol mechanism.**

- Evidence: Lines 173-175: "2. Andere Clients lesen dies und wissen:\n   - Wo sie deine Posts finden (read relays)\n   - Wohin sie dir Nachrichten senden (write relays)". NIP-65 (nostr-protocol/nips/65.md) says the opposite: "When downloading events from a user, clients SHOULD use the write relays of that user" and mentions/tags go to the user's read relays. Note: the EN source (en/relays-demystified.mdx lines 173-176) carries the same inversion; the DE file faithfully copies it.
- Fix: Swap the two labels in the code block: "- Wo sie deine Posts finden (write relays)" and "- Wohin sie Erwähnungen und Antworten für dich senden (read relays)". Apply the identical swap in the EN source file (and check pl/es/zh/ar/hi, which likely mirror it).
**5. [src/content/guides/en/faq.mdx:726] The DM privacy answer inverts what relays can see under NIP-17: it promises the recipient is hidden, when the recipient is exactly what relays DO see (the sender is what is hidden).**

- Evidence: "Relay operators can see you're messaging but not content or exact recipient with NIP-17." (in 'Are my DMs actually private?', line 726). NIP-17/59: the kind 1059 gift wrap carries the receiver's pubkey in a plaintext p tag ("Relays SHOULD protect message metadata by only serving kind:1059 events to users p-tagged on the event") and is signed by a random one-time key, so the sender is invisible. The site's own nip17-private-messages.mdx says it outright (lines 38-40): "NIP-17 hides the **sender**, not the **recipient**."
- Fix: Rewrite the sentence to: "Relay operators can't see who sent a message (each one is wrapped and signed with a throwaway key), but they can see which pubkey it is addressed to, plus your IP if you connect directly. Content stays encrypted either way." Keep the existing VPN/Tor callout. Also mirror the wording used in /guides/nip17-private-messages so the two pages agree.
**6. [src/content/guides/en/faq.mdx:726] FAQ promises NIP-17 hides the DM recipient; the NIP-17 guide and the spec say the recipient stays visible.**

- Evidence: "Relay operators can see you're messaging but not content or exact recipient with NIP-17." (line 726, 'Are my DMs actually private?'). Also line 674: "NIP-17 hides metadata about who is messaging whom" and the table row "Metadata protection | ❌ No | ✅ Yes" (line 681). nip17-private-messages.mdx has a dedicated warning callout: "The gift wrap carries the recipient's public key in a plaintext `p` tag... NIP-17 hides the **sender**, not the **recipient**." NIP-17 spec (fetched 2026-09-01) confirms: tags: ["p", receiverPublicKey, ...].
- Fix: Rewrite both FAQ answers to mirror the nip17 guide's model: NIP-17 hides the sender (gift wrap signed by a one-time key) and the content, but the recipient's pubkey sits in a plaintext p tag, so relays can see that someone is messaging you and when. Change the table row to "Sender hidden ✅ / Recipient still visible ⚠️" and delete "not... exact recipient". Link to /guides/nip17-private-messages for the limits. As written this is a privacy promise a user could act on and be burned by.
**7. [src/content/guides/en/finding-community.mdx:441] "Block - They can't interact with you" overstates what blocking does on Nostr and gives harassment targets a false sense of protection.**

- Evidence: "2. **Block** - They can't interact with you" (section "DM Etiquette > Handling Unwanted DMs", line 441). There is no protocol-level block on Nostr: block/mute is client-side filtering (NIP-51 mute lists). A blocked person can still read your public posts, reply, mention and zap you; you stop seeing it, but everyone else still does.
- Fix: Reword to match reality, e.g.: "Block/mute - hides their posts, replies and DMs from you. On Nostr this is a client-side filter: they can still see your public posts and reply where others may see it, but they lose your attention. For persistent harassment, also use your client's report feature and consider a paid or moderated relay."
**8. [src/content/guides/en/multi-client.mdx:292] The guide teaches pasting your nsec into every new client, including web clients, and never mentions signers**

- Evidence: Backup & Migration, 'Easy (automatic)' steps (lines 289-292): '1. Install new client 2. Import your nsec 3. Wait for sync'. Every recommended desktop pairing is a web client ('Desktop: Iris (web) or Snort (web)', line 156; 'Iris (web) or Coracle (web)', line 162), so the instruction amounts to pasting nsec into websites. The prerequisite guide keys-and-security.mdx says 'NEVER paste it into random websites' (line 86) and recommends Amber signer (line 242). Even this guide's own quiz contradicts the body: en.json guides.multiClient.quiz question 'security-multiple' teaches 'Use a signer app so you don't paste your nsec into every client' as the correct answer, while question 'same-identity' has the correct answer labeled 'Yes, your keys work everywhere - import your nsec into any client' with explanation 'Import your nsec into any Nostr client (Damus, Amethyst, Iris, etc.)'. The words 'signer', 'extension', 'Amber', 'NIP-07' appear nowhere in the mdx body.
- Fix: Rewrite 'Moving Between Clients' to lead with signers: on web clients sign in with a NIP-07 browser extension (Alby or nos2x); on Android use Amber (the client asks Amber to sign, your nsec never enters the app); paste nsec directly only into a well-known app-store client as a last resort, and never into a website. Change step 2 from 'Import your nsec' to 'Sign in with your signer (or, on a trusted store app, your nsec)'. Add one Callout linking to the signer section of /guides/keys-and-security. In src/i18n/locales/en.json, reword the 'same-identity' question's correct option to 'Yes - one identity works in every client (sign in with a signer app or extension)' and drop 'import your nsec into any client' from its explanation; mirror the change in the other six locale files.
**9. [src/content/guides/en/multi-client.mdx:290] Multi-client guide instructs importing nsec into every new client, including the web clients it recommends, contradicting quickstart's never-paste-nsec-into-websites rule.**

- Evidence: "**Easy (automatic):** 1. Install new client 2. Import your nsec 3. Wait for sync" (Backup & Migration, lines ~289-294) and "**Don't:** Import nsec into sketchy clients" (line ~335), while the guide's pairings push web clients: "Desktop: Iris (web) or Snort (web)", "Desktop: Iris (web) or Coracle (web)". quickstart.mdx teaches the opposite: "**In a web browser, do not paste your nsec into the website.** Install a signer extension instead" and "A website that asks for your nsec is a red flag". multi-client never mentions NIP-07, extensions, or Amber, even though one-key-many-clients is exactly the signer use case privacy-security.mdx describes ("Use same keys across apps").
- Fix: In 'Backup & Migration' and 'Testing New Clients', split the login step by platform, reusing quickstart's rule: mobile app = paste nsec once (sandboxed); web/desktop client = connect via a NIP-07 signer extension (Alby, nos2x, Nostore) or NIP-46 remote signer (nsec.app), never paste the nsec into the site; Android = Amber. Change "Import nsec into sketchy clients" to "paste your nsec into any website" and add a one-line pointer to /guides/quickstart and the signer section of /guides/privacy-security.
**10. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:158] Damus is presented as supporting NIP-17 'Default for new DMs' when Damus still ships NIP-04-only DMs, giving Damus users a false privacy promise**

- Evidence: Step 1 table: "| Damus    | Yes            | Default for new DMs |" (line 158); Step 2: "Toggle \"Use NIP-17 for new messages\" to ON" (line 172); Client-Specific section: "Damus made NIP-17 the default for new direct messages in recent versions" (line 238) and "Damus shows a lock icon with \"NIP-17\" text on secure messages" (line 244). Verified false as of 2026-09: Damus README Spec Compliance lists "NIP-04: Encrypted direct message" and no NIP-17/NIP-44; the full CHANGELOG through v1.17 (2026-06-03) contains zero mentions of NIP-17, gift wrap, kind 1059, or NIP-44; feature request damus-io/damus#2815 ('nip-17 DM - remove metadata leaks') is still open. A reader on Damus will believe sender/recipient metadata is hidden while their client actually publishes kind-4 events exposing both.
- Fix: Remove Damus from the 'Yes' rows and delete both Damus walkthroughs (lines 168-175 and 236-244). Add one honest sentence: as of mid-2026 Damus DMs still use deprecated NIP-04, so sender and recipient are visible to relays; use a NIP-17 client (e.g. Amethyst, 0xchat, Coracle, Nos) for metadata-protected DMs, and check damus-io/damus issue #2815 for status.
**11. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:84] False privacy promise that NIP-17 hides recipient information, contradicting the NIP-17 spec and the guide's own Callout**

- Evidence: Line 84: "NIP-17 solves this by wrapping the entire message structure in an additional encryption layer, making it impossible to see sender or recipient information without the decryption key." Repeated in the Conclusion, line 370: "it enables truly private direct messaging that protects both content and communication patterns." Per NIP-17 the gift wrap's p tag carries the receiver's public key in plaintext, and the guide's own Callout (lines 37-46), table row 58 and Key Benefit 1 (lines 119-121) say exactly that. Two of the guide's load-bearing sentences promise recipient-hiding that does not exist.
- Fix: Line 84: change to "...making it impossible to see who SENT a message. The recipient stays visible in the gift wrap's p tag, as the callout above explains." Line 370: change "protects both content and communication patterns" to "protects message content and hides the sender; who receives messages, and when, remains observable."
**12. [src/content/guides/en/privacy-security.mdx:273] The NIP-05 callout claims followers automatically follow your new npub after repointing the identifier, which NIP-05 explicitly forbids clients to do**

- Evidence: "**Pro tip:** If you have a NIP-05, you can update it to point to your new npub. People using your NIP-05 will automatically follow your new account." (Callout at end of 'Key Rotation' section, lines 273-276). NIP-05 spec says the opposite under 'Clients must always follow public keys, not NIP-05 addresses': the client must keep the pubkey as primary reference and "must not replace" it if the address starts returning a different key. There is no auto-follow mechanism; follows live in kind:3 lists as pubkeys.
- Fix: Rewrite the callout to the truthful version: updating your NIP-05 helps people who SEARCH your identifier find the new account and stops the old account from showing your verified name, but existing followers stay pinned to the old pubkey and must re-follow manually; the pinned announcement post from the old account is the real migration path. A creator rotating keys on the current advice would silently lose their whole audience.
**13. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:249] Guide teaches that switching Nostr clients means importing your nsec, with no signer caveat, while recommending a web client in the same guide**

- Evidence: "- ✅ Switch clients instantly: just import your nsec" (Data Portability > Nostr, line 249). The same guide recommends "Iris (Web)" at line 340, so a beginner following this bullet pastes their nsec into a website. The site's own quickstart.mdx (lines 177-180) says the opposite: "In a web browser, do not paste your nsec into the website. Install a signer"; privacy-security.mdx teaches Amber signer flow where "Client app never sees your nsec".
- Fix: Replace line 249 with: "- ✅ Switch clients instantly: log in with your keys — on a phone via a signer app like Amber, in a browser via a signer extension. Never paste your nsec directly into a website." Propagate the same change to the six translations of this guide.
**14. [src/content/guides/en/relays-demystified.mdx:174] NIP-65 read/write relay semantics are taught exactly inverted, contradicting both NIP-65 and the site's own outbox-model guide.**

- Evidence: "2. Other clients read this and know:\n   - Where to find your posts (read relays)\n   - Where to send you messages (write relays)" (code block in 'How It Works', lines 173-176). NIP-65 says the opposite: "When downloading events from a user, clients SHOULD use the write relays of that user" and events tagging a user are sent to that user's READ relays. The site's own outbox-model.mdx line 78 has it right: "**write** → Where you post your content (outbox)". A reader marking relays read/write in a client (Amethyst, etc.) based on this guide configures their relay list backwards and becomes undiscoverable.
- Fix: Rewrite the 'How It Works' block: write relays = where YOU publish and where others go to FETCH your posts; read relays = where others SEND events that mention or tag you (replies, DM-adjacent notifications) and where you check for them. Drop the invented {"read":[...],"write":[...]} JSON (NIP-65 is a kind 10002 event with r tags, but for this audience skip the format entirely) and end the section with a link to the site's own /guides/outbox-model instead of re-deriving it here.
**15. [src/content/guides/en/relays-demystified.mdx:173] NIP-65 read/write semantics are inverted, contradicting the outbox-model guide and the spec.**

- Evidence: "2. Other clients read this and know:\n   - Where to find your posts (read relays)\n   - Where to send you messages (write relays)" (section 'Advanced: Outbox Model (NIP-65)', lines ~166-176). outbox-model.mdx teaches the opposite and correctly: "**write** → Where you post your content (outbox) / **read** → Where you check for mentions/replies (inbox)". NIP-65 (fetched 2026-09-01): "When downloading events FROM a user, clients SHOULD use the WRITE relays of that user" and mentions go to READ relays. The example JSON `{"read": [...], "write": [...]}` also invents a structure that is not the kind:10002 r-tag format shown in outbox-model.mdx.
- Fix: Swap the two bullets: write relays = where you publish, so followers fetch your posts there; read relays = where people send your mentions/replies. Replace the fake `{read:[], write:[]}` JSON with the kind:10002 r-tag example already used in outbox-model.mdx (tags: ["r", "wss://...", "write"|"read"]), or shorten the whole section to two sentences plus a link to /guides/outbox-model as the canonical explanation. A reader who applies the current mapping in a client marks their relays backwards and their posts become undiscoverable.
**16. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:214] Tells beginners to clear app data with an assurance of safety, omitting that this deletes the stored nsec and permanently locks out anyone without a key backup**

- Evidence: "- **Solution**: Clear app data (you won't lose posts/follows)" — under 'Problem: "Client Crashes"', '3. Corrupted cache', line 214. The same guide's 'Lost My Keys' section (line 239) states the consequence: "If you don't have a backup of your keys, your account is unrecoverable." On Android, Settings > Apps > Clear data wipes the locally stored private key.
- Fix: Rewrite the bullet to: "**First**: Make sure you have your nsec backed up (Settings → Keys) — clearing data logs you out and removes the stored key. **Then**: Clear app data; your posts and follows live on relays and return when you log back in." Apply the same caveat to the 'Nuclear option: Reinstall app, re-import keys' line.

An `npub` here is the single most common cause of a NIP-05 that never verifies." The ES page's own warning callout (line 240) correctly says npub in nostr.json fails silently, so the troubleshooting section contradicts the callout and validates the broken setup for exactly the reader who lands there with a failing NIP-05.

Un `npub` aquí es la causa más común de un NIP-05 que nunca se verifica."
**18. [src/content/guides/pl/nip05-identity.mdx:311] Troubleshooting for 'NIP-05 nie działa' still tells the reader to check their npub inside nostr.json, teaching the exact broken setup the rewritten EN bullet warns against.**

- Evidence: Line 311: "- Sprawdź dwukrotnie, czy Twój npub jest poprawny w nostr.json". EN (en/nip05-identity.mdx lines 311-313) was rewritten to: "**Check the key format first.** `nostr.json` must contain your public key in lowercase hex — 64 characters, no `npub1` prefix. An `npub` here is the single most common cause of a NIP-05 that never verifies." The PL bullet presupposes an npub belongs in nostr.json and contradicts the (correctly translated) hex Callout at line 240 of the same file, so a reader debugging a failure is steered into keeping the broken configuration.
- Fix: Replace line 311 with a translation of the current EN bullet: "- **Najpierw sprawdź format klucza.** `nostr.json` musi zawierać Twój klucz publiczny w postaci hex małymi literami — 64 znaki, bez prefiksu `npub1`. Wklejony `npub` to najczęstsza przyczyna NIP-05, który nigdy się nie weryfikuje."
**19. [src/content/guides/pl/nip17-private-messages.mdx:120] The 'Kluczowe Korzyści' list is a stale translation that makes two false security claims the updated EN source explicitly denies (recipient anonymity and forward secrecy).**

- Evidence: Lines 120-122: "1. **Prywatność Metadanych**: Nikt oprócz odbiorcy nie może ustalić kto wysłał wiadomość lub komu została wysłana" and "3. **Lepsza Forward Secrecy**: Każda wiadomość używa unikalnych kluczy efemerycznych". EN (en/nip17-private-messages.mdx lines 119-127) now says the opposite: "The recipient, however, is named in a plaintext `p` tag that relays must be able to read" and "**No forward secrecy**: ... an attacker who later obtains your `nsec` can decrypt every message they saved." The PL claim even contradicts the correctly translated Callout on line 38 of the same file ("Relaye nadal widzą, do kogo piszesz").
- Fix: Replace the three-item list under '### Kluczowe Korzyści' with a translation of the current EN list: 1. "**Prywatność nadawcy**: Nikt poza odbiorcą nie ustali, kto wysłał wiadomość. Odbiorca jest jednak wskazany w jawnym tagu `p`, który relaye muszą móc odczytać." 2. "**Niepowiązywalność**: Każda wiadomość jest podpisana świeżym kluczem jednorazowym, więc wiadomości od tego samego nadawcy nie układają się w oczywisty zbiór." 3. "**Brak forward secrecy**: NIP-44 wyprowadza klucz rozmowy z dwóch statycznych kluczy, więc atakujący, który później zdobędzie Twój `nsec`, odszyfruje każdą zapisaną wiadomość. Klucze jednorazowe ukrywają, *kto wysłał* wiadomość; nie chronią starych wiadomości. Jeśli to dla Ciebie istotne, usuwaj stare rozmowy."
**20. [src/content/guides/zh/nip05-identity.mdx:308] Troubleshooting bullet tells the reader to verify their npub is in nostr.json — the exact broken setup the EN source was rewritten to warn against (nostr.json requires the hex key, not npub).**

- Evidence: Line 308, under "NIP-05 无法工作": "- 仔细检查你的 npub 在 nostr.json 中是否正确". EN source bullet: "**Check the key format first.** `nostr.json` must contain your public key in lowercase hex — 64 characters, no `npub1` prefix. An `npub` here is the single most common cause of a NIP-05 that never verifies." A zh reader debugging a failed NIP-05 is told to confirm the npub is present — perpetuating the silent-failure setup the page's own warning Callout (line 239) forbids.

**21. [src/content/guides/zh/nip17-private-messages.mdx:51] Comparison table claims NIP-17 hides recipient metadata and provides forward secrecy — both false and both explicitly corrected in the EN source.**

- Evidence: Line 51: "| **接收者元数据** | 对中继可见 | 对除接收者外的所有人隐藏 |"; line 54: "| **前向保密** | 否 | 更好（每条消息使用不同密钥） |"; line 53: "| **重放保护** | 无 | 内置时间戳 |". EN source table says Recipient Metadata: "**Still visible** — plaintext `p` tag", Forward Secrecy: "No — NIP-44 does not provide it", Replay Protection: "Randomised timestamps". The zh table even contradicts the correct Callout 15 lines above it ("中继仍然看得到你在跟谁发消息").
- Fix: Replace the three stale table rows to mirror EN: 发送者元数据 → "隐藏——由一次性密钥签名"; 接收者元数据 → "**仍然可见**——明文 `p` 标签"; 重放保护 → "随机化时间戳"; 前向保密 → "否——NIP-44 不提供前向保密".
**22. [src/content/guides/zh/nip17-private-messages.mdx:112] "主要优势" list keeps the pre-correction claims: nobody can see who a message was sent to, and "better forward secrecy" — the EN source now says the opposite.**

- Evidence: Lines 112–114: "1. **元数据隐私**：除了接收者，没人能确定谁发送了消息或发送给谁 ... 3. **更好的前向保密**：每条消息使用唯一的临时密钥". EN Key Benefits: "1. **Sender privacy** ... The recipient, however, is named in a plaintext `p` tag that relays must be able to read. ... 3. **No forward secrecy**: NIP-44 derives its conversation key from two static keys, so an attacker who later obtains your `nsec` can decrypt every message they saved. ... Delete old conversations if that matters to you." The zh drops an actionable security warning and inverts it into a false privacy promise.
- Fix: Retranslate the three items from current EN: 1. 发送者隐私（接收者除外没人能确定发送者，但接收者写在中继必须能读取的明文 `p` 标签里）；2. 不可链接性（每条消息由全新的一次性密钥签名，同一发送者的消息不会明显关联）；3. **没有前向保密**（NIP-44 的会话密钥由两把静态密钥派生，日后拿到你 nsec 的攻击者可解密其保存的所有历史消息；一次性密钥只隐藏发送者，不保护旧消息——在意就删除旧对话）。
**23. [src/i18n/locales/en.json:978] Marked-correct answer instructs importing your nsec into any client, contradicting the site's own key-safety rule.**

- Evidence: guides.multiClient.quiz.questions[0] (id same-identity): correct option label "Yes, your keys work everywhere - import your nsec into any client" (en.json line 978) and explanation "Import your nsec into any Nostr client (Damus, Amethyst, Iris, etc.)..." (line 986). Three questions later the same quiz teaches "Use a signer app so you don't paste your nsec into every client" (line ~1040), and the security quiz explanation at line 216 says "Never paste your main private key into new clients." A beginner reading the marked-correct answer literally will paste their nsec into arbitrary apps.
- Fix: Reword the correct option to "Yes — one identity works in every client; connect new clients through a signer app instead of pasting your nsec into each one" and the explanation to: "Your identity is portable across clients (Damus, Amethyst, etc.). Log in via a signer (Amber on Android, extension signers on web) or, for a client you fully trust, your nsec — never paste it into apps you haven't vetted."
**24. [src/i18n/locales/hi.json:1224] Hindi-only privacy quiz explanation claims Nostr message content is encrypted, which is false for regular posts**

- Evidence: guides.privacySecurity.quiz.questions[0].explanation (id "ip-exposure", line 1224): "रिले आपकी IP पता देख सकते हैं जब आप कनेक्ट होते हैं। VPN या Tor का उपयोग करके, आप इस जानकारी को सीमित कर सकते हैं। संदेश सामग्री एन्क्रिप्टेड है, लेकिन मेटाडेटा नहीं।" The last sentence ("message content is encrypted, but metadata is not") is a blanket claim; ordinary Nostr notes are plaintext and fully readable by relays - only NIP-17/NIP-04 DMs are encrypted. This question exists only in hi (en has "separate-personas"/"exif-data" here), so the false sentence has no English source. A creator reading this may post sensitive content believing it is encrypted.
- Fix: Replace the last sentence of the explanation with: "ध्यान रखें: आपकी सामान्य पोस्ट सार्वजनिक होती हैं और रिले उन्हें पूरा पढ़ सकते हैं - केवल निजी संदेश (DM) एन्क्रिप्टेड होते हैं।" While editing this question, also fix the gender error "आपकी IP पता" -> "आपका IP पता" in options[1].label (line 1216: "आपकी IP पता और कनेक्शन टाइम स्टैंप" -> "आपका IP पता और कनेक्शन टाइमस्टैम्प") and in the explanation's first sentence ("रिले आपकी IP पता" -> "रिले आपका IP पता").
**25. [src/i18n/locales/zh.json:1409] The zh NIP-17 quiz teaches, as marked-correct answers, that gift wrap hides the recipient and that relays cannot tell a private message was sent — both false per NIP-17/NIP-59, where the recipient's pubkey is a plaintext p tag on the kind 1059 gift wrap and only the sender is hidden.**

- Evidence: guides.nip17PrivateMessages.quiz.questions[2].explanation (line 1409): "礼品包装将密封事件包装在另一层加密中。这使中继和旁观者无法判断谁在发送消息或发送给谁。"; questions[3].options[0].label, the CORRECT option (line 1419): "只有发送者和接收者——中继只看到匿名礼品包装"; questions[3].explanation (line 1431): "NIP-17 设计使中继甚至不知道消息是私信。它们看起来像匿名事件。"; also questions[2].options[0].label (line 1397): "隐藏发送者身份和对话事实". NIP-59 specifies the gift wrap carries ["p", "<recipient pubkey>"] in plaintext and is signed by a random one-time key, so relays see exactly who receives DMs; NIP-17 only mitigates this via optional NIP-42 AUTH on relays. The current EN quiz states it correctly: "The relay sees the encrypted message and knows who should receive it, but not who sent it." A zh reader in a hostile environment could rely on receiving DMs being invisible; it is not.
- Fix: Replace the zh nip17PrivateMessages quiz with a translation of the current en.json question set (nip17-purpose, nip04-vs-nip17, migration, sealed-sender, client-support), whose sealed-sender wording is accurate. If patching in place instead: line 1409 -> "这使中继和旁观者无法判断消息是谁发送的；不过接收者的公钥仍以 p 标签明文出现在礼品包装上，用于投递。"; line 1419 -> "中继看不到发送者，但能看到礼品包装发给了哪个公钥" (and adjust the question prompt so this remains the clearly correct option); line 1431 -> "中继知道某人收到了加密的礼品包装事件，但无法得知是谁发送的，也无法读取内容。只有预期的接收者可以解包和阅读。"

### high
**26. [src/content/guides/ar/faq.mdx:210] Dropped negation inverts "you're never trapped in one app" into "you are trapped forever in one app".**

- Evidence: Line 210: "هذا هو سبب قوة Nostr—أنت محاصر أبداً في تطبيق واحد." EN (line 211): "This is why Nostr is powerful—you're never trapped in one app." Arabic "أبداً" needs an explicit negation (لست/لا); without it the sentence reads as the affirmative "you are forever trapped in one app", the opposite of the selling point being made.
- Fix: Change to: "هذا هو سبب قوة Nostr — لست محاصرًا أبدًا في تطبيق واحد."
**27. [src/content/guides/ar/finding-community.mdx:94] "Relay" is translated as "الترحيل" (deportation/relocation) throughout this guide, contradicting the site UI and the other three Arabic guides which keep "Relay" in English**

- Evidence: Heading line 94: "## استكشاف مغذيات الترحيل"; line 100: "**فكر في الترحيلات كصحف مختلفة:**"; line 135: "### الترحيل الموصى به للبدء"; ~25 occurrences of الترحيل/الترحيلات in lines 94-153 and 277. Meanwhile ar/what-is-nostr.mdx uses "Relays = مكاتب البريد" (line 113) and ar.json keeps "Relay" in Latin 275 times, per the convention in CONTENT_TRANSLATION.md ("relay — Keep English") and LESSONS_AR_LOCALE.md ("Technical terms may stay in English (Bitcoin, Nostr, Relay, Zap)").
- Fix: Replace every الترحيل/الترحيلات/ترحيل with "Relay"/"Relays" (Latin), e.g. "## استكشاف خلاصات الـ Relays", "فكر في الـ Relays كصحف مختلفة", "الـ Relay الموصى به للبدء", "معلومات الـ Relays" (line 277). Combine with the feed-term fix: "مغذيات الترحيل" → "خلاصات الـ Relays", "مغذى الترحيل" → "خلاصة الـ Relay".
**28. [src/content/guides/ar/finding-community.mdx:456] The term "zap" is corrupted into the mixed-script token "زap"/"زaps" (Arabic ز glued to Latin "ap"), which renders as bidi garbage**

- Evidence: Line 456: "- 80% إضافة قيمة (منشورات، ردود، زaps)"; line 488: "- زap محتواهم"; line 527: "- زap بسخاء". The Arabic letter ز replaced Latin "z", producing an unreadable mixed-direction token. Contrast ar/quickstart.mdx line 250 which correctly keeps "من zaps إلى الخصوصية".
- Fix: Replace with the Latin term per project convention: line 456 "(منشورات، ردود، zaps)", line 488 "- أرسل zap لمحتواهم", line 527 "- أرسل zaps بسخاء". (Same corruption exists in ar/multi-client.mdx line 176 "الزaps" — outside this task's four guides but worth the same fix.)
**29. [src/content/guides/ar/multi-client.mdx:126] "NIP-51 يقيّد هذا" says NIP-51 restricts mute/block list portability; EN says NIP-51 is standardizing it.**

- Evidence: Line 126 under "الكتم/الحظر": "- NIP-51 يقيّد هذا". EN (line 127): "NIP-51 standardizing this". "يقيّد" means "restricts/constrains", so the translation asserts the opposite purpose of the NIP.
- Fix: Change to: "- معيار NIP-51 يعمل على توحيد هذا" (or "NIP-51 يوحّد هذا قياسيًا").
**30. [src/content/guides/ar/outbox-model.mdx:63] The core kind:10002 JSON example and ASCII flow diagrams render visually scrambled because ar pages give code blocks an inherited RTL base direction.**

- Evidence: The site sets dir="rtl" on <html> for ar (src/layouts/Layout.astro:32) and nothing overrides direction on pre/.astro-code (checked src/styles/globals.css). Unicode BIDI reordering (verified with python-bidi, base_dir='R') shows the jsonc fence at lines 61-74 displays '"kind": 10002,' as ',kind": 10002"' and '["r", "wss://relay.nostr.info", "write"],' with the comma jumped to the line start; scenario lines like '  - wss://relay.a.com [write]' (line 89) display with broken/mirrored brackets; diagram arrows in 'أنت تنشر → Relay A → ❌ صديقك يستخدم Relay B فقط' (line 19) point rightward against the right-to-left reading flow. The privacy-security Arabic alert template was verified to render correctly under RTL, so a blanket LTR override on all pre would break it.
- Fix: In src/content/guides/ar/outbox-model.mdx, wrap each fenced block in a raw <div dir="ltr"> ... </div> (MDX allows this; keep blank lines around the fence): the jsonc block at 61-74, the diagrams at 18-23 and 31-36, the scenario blocks at 87-96, 100-110, 114-122, and the example at 166-169. Under an LTR base the JSON renders as valid syntax and the → arrows point along the flow again (embedded Arabic runs still read RTL internally). Do NOT add global CSS forcing LTR on all code blocks — the Arabic security-alert template in ar/privacy-security.mdx (lines 444-456) renders correctly only under RTL. Check the other ar guides' json/jsonc fences for the same treatment in a follow-up.

**35. [src/content/guides/en/faq.mdx:205] The client-switching walkthrough teaches export-and-paste of the nsec into every new client (including the web clients this FAQ recommends) with no mention of signers, contradicting the site's own keys-and-security best practices.**

Clients request signatures instead of storing keys".

**38. [src/content/guides/en/faq.mdx:162] All three 'Specialized Clients' entries are wrong: noStrudel's specialty is invented, Plebstr no longer exists under that name, and Current is abandoned with a fabricated description.**

Plebstr was rebranded into the multi-network client Openvibe (Nostr+Bluesky+Mastodon). Current's last release was Sept 2023 and it was a general social+wallet client, never music-focused.

**39. [src/content/guides/en/faq.mdx:566] The identity-verification answer presents the NIP-05 checkmark as a verification badge, but NIP-05 explicitly does not verify identity and anyone can buy any name from a provider, so this teaches a beginner to trust impersonators.**

- Evidence: "Check if their profile has a verification badge." (line 566) and "1. **NIP-05 identifier** - Look for the checkmark badge" (line 570) in 'How do I verify someone's identity?'. NIP-05 spec: "The NIP-05 is not intended to _verify_ a user, but only to _identify_ them". An impersonator can register famousname@nostrplebs.com and get the same checkmark.
- Fix: Reword method 1: the checkmark only proves that a domain maps that name to that key, so it is meaningful only when you trust the domain (a creator's own website, their employer), and a name bought from a public NIP-05 provider proves nothing about who the person is. Keep methods 2-4 and the impersonation callout, which are good.

**44. [src/content/guides/en/keys-and-security.mdx:307] Case Study 1 teaches that zap sats live in the nsec, which is false per NIP-57 and could lead creators to mis-secure their actual money.**

- Evidence: "A content creator received $50,000 worth of Bitcoin tips (zaps) over 6 months. Their phone was stolen—they had no backup. The tips were gone forever because the nsec was only on that device." (Advanced: Real-World Cautionary Tales, Case Study 1, ~line 307)
- Fix: Rewrite the case study so the loss is the identity, not the money: per NIP-57 zap sats are paid to the recipient's Lightning wallet via their lnurl/lightning address and are never custodied by the Nostr private key. Either make the anecdote about losing followers/identity, or state explicitly that the money was lost because the Lightning wallet app on the stolen phone had no wallet backup (a separate backup from the nsec). Never imply backing up the nsec protects zap funds.

- Evidence: Client-Specific Features (lines 249-254): '**Coracle (Web):** - Matrix integration - Unique features - Good performance - Active development'. Coracle's README (github.com/coracle-social/coracle, fetched 2026-09-01) describes 'relay selection and management, web-of-trust based moderation and content recommendations, and privacy protection' and contains no mention of Matrix at all. This matches the July audit's 'invented product descriptions' pattern.

**46. [src/content/guides/en/multi-client.mdx:86] DMs listed under 'Universal (works across all clients)' sync, which NIP-17 delivery makes false**

- Evidence: Syncing Strategy, 'What Syncs Automatically' (lines 79-86): '**Universal (works across all clients):** ... - ✓ DMs (if properly encrypted)'. Per NIP-17 (fetched from nostr-protocol/nips), gift-wrapped DMs are published only to the relays in the recipient's kind 10050 list and are readable only by clients that implement NIP-17; a legacy NIP-04-only client shows a different, disjoint DM history. The hedge '(if properly encrypted)' names the wrong condition - encryption is not what gates syncing, protocol support and DM relays are. The site's own nip17-private-messages.mdx has a per-client support table, which this 'all clients' claim contradicts.
- Fix: Move DMs out of the 'Universal' list into a caveated bullet: 'DMs - sync only between clients that support the same DM standard (modern private DMs, NIP-17) and are connected to your DM relays; older clients may show a different conversation history.' Link it to /guides/nip17-private-messages.
**47. [src/content/guides/en/nip05-identity.mdx:195] "Other Paid Providers" recommends two nonexistent services (NostrName.com, NostrID.com) plus an unverifiable registrar claim.**

- Evidence: "- **NostrName.com** - Various pricing tiers\n- **NostrID.com** - Domain + NIP-05 packages\n- **Custom domain registrars** - Many now offer NIP-05 setup" (~lines 195-197). Verified 2026-09-01: both domains fail DNS (ENOTFOUND); no mainstream registrar (Namecheap, Porkbun, Cloudflare) offers NIP-05 setup, so the third bullet is fabricated too.
- Fix: Delete the entire "Other Paid Providers" bullet list. Keep NostrPlebs (verified live, pricing confirmed) as the paid example and keep the "What you get" list and comparison table that follow. If a second paid option is wanted, verify one first (nostraddress.com advertises free+premium tiers but was not verified in this audit).
**48. [src/content/guides/en/nip05-identity.mdx:86] The Trust & Authenticity section overstates what the checkmark proves; NIP-05 is identification, not verification, and an impostor can get a badge on any free provider.**

- Evidence: "Many clients show a ✓ badge for NIP-05 users ... This helps people know they're following the real you, not an impostor." (~lines 76-86), reinforced by "**Trust signal**: NIP-05 badge in many clients" (line 42). NIP-05 spec (05.md) states it provides identification only, not verification, and that trust derives from the user's relationship with the domain. Anyone can register an unused name like yourname@nostrcheck.me and display the same ✓, so a beginner taught "badge = real person" can be fooled by a verified impostor.
- Fix: After the MockProfile, add a caveat paragraph: the ✓ only proves that name@domain points to that key. The trust comes from the domain, not the badge — alice@alice.dev means the owner of alice.dev vouches for that key, while alice@ any open free provider means only that the name was unclaimed. Advise readers to check WHICH domain a badge shows before trusting it, and soften line 42 to something like "Trust signal — when the domain itself is trustworthy".
**49. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:222] Verification step tells readers the p tag should be a random ephemeral key, which is false and guarantees every correct setup looks broken**

- Evidence: Step 5, line 222: "4. The `p` tag should point to a random ephemeral key, not your friend's real key". Per NIP-17 the gift wrap's p tag contains "the receiver's public key and relay URL"; it is the `pubkey` field that is the random one-time key. The guide's own JSON example four lines below says so: "\"pubkey\": \"<random_ephemeral_key>\", // Not the real sender!" and "\"tags\": [[\"p\", \"<recipient_pubkey>\"]]" (lines 228-229). A reader who checks will always see the recipient's real key in the p tag and conclude their migration failed.
- Fix: Replace line 222 with: "4. The `pubkey` field should be a random key you don't recognize (not your own and not your friend's); the `p` tag will still show the recipient — that is expected and required for delivery."
**50. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:92] The seal/gift-wrap mechanism description misassigns the keys of both layers, inverting how NIP-17 actually works**

- Evidence: Line 92: gift wrap "encrypted to a special \"gift wrap key\" derived from the recipient's public key" (no such key exists; it is NIP-44 encryption with a conversation key from the random wrapper key + recipient pubkey). Line 103: the seal "Is encrypted using a fresh ephemeral key pair" (false: the seal is SIGNED by the sender's real key and encrypted with the sender-recipient conversation key; the ephemeral key belongs to the gift wrap). Lines 25-26: "**Seal** (kind 13) — an inner layer that encrypts the message and hides which key signed it" (inverted: the seal PROVES the sender by its real-key signature; the gift wrap is what hides it).
- Fix: Rewrite the three spots per NIP-17: seal (kind 13) = signed by the sender's real key, content NIP-44-encrypted with a key derived from sender's private key + recipient's public key, proving to the recipient who wrote it; gift wrap (kind 1059) = signed by a fresh random key, content NIP-44-encrypted with a key from that random key + recipient's public key, hiding the seal (and thus the sender) from relays. Fix the intro bullet to say the seal proves authorship and the gift wrap hides it.
**51. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:166] Two duplicated, mutually contradictory client walkthrough sections with invented settings paths and features that cannot be verified in any client**

- Evidence: Step 2 (lines 166-187) and "Client-Specific Setup Guides" (lines 234-263) walk through the same three clients with DIFFERENT menu paths: Amethyst "Settings → Messages → Enable 'Use NIP-17 for DMs' → Restart the app" (179-181) vs "Settings → Advanced → Protocol Settings → Enable 'NIP-17 gift wrap for DMs'" (250-251); Primal "Settings → Privacy" (185) vs "Settings → Security & Privacy" (260) plus an invented "Auto-upgrade NIP-04 conversations" toggle (262). Also "Amethyst gracefully falls back to NIP-04 if the recipient doesn't support NIP-17" (254) asserted without basis (and a silent NIP-04 fallback would itself be a metadata leak worth warning about, not praising). Amethyst's own README lists "Private Direct Messages (NIP-17)" as built-in with no enable toggle documented. Iris and Snort table rows (162-163) are unverified; only Nos checks out (Nos publicly states "We use NIP-17 as our default DM type").
- Fix: Delete both walkthrough sections and the per-client table rows that cannot be verified. Replace with one section saying: clients that support NIP-17 use it automatically with other NIP-17 clients, there is usually nothing to toggle; keep a short verified list (Amethyst, 0xchat, Coracle, Nos) with a 'check your client's own docs, menus change' caveat, and keep only the genuinely universal steps: publish the kind 10050 list and verify delivery with a test message.
**52. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:359] Future section claims NIP-44 'may supersede NIP-17', but NIP-44 is the encryption NIP-17 already uses**

- Evidence: Line 359: "- **NIP-44**: An improved encryption scheme that may supersede NIP-17". The NIP-17 spec states it "uses NIP-44 encryption and NIP-59 gift-wrapping", and this guide itself says so at line 124 ("NIP-44 derives its conversation key..."). The two are complementary layers, not competitors.
- Fix: Replace the bullet with the actual 2026 direction: MLS-based encrypted messaging on Nostr (NIP-EE / the Marmot protocol, implemented in clients like White Noise), which adds forward secrecy and scalable group chats that NIP-17 lacks. Fold the existing "Group encryption" bullet into it.
**53. [src/content/guides/en/nip17-private-messages.mdx:223] Step 5's verification bullet says the gift wrap p tag is a random ephemeral key, contradicting the guide's own callout, its own example JSON, and NIP-17.**

- Evidence: "4. The `p` tag should point to a random ephemeral key, not your friend's real key" (Step 5: Verify Migration Success, line 223). Six lines later the guide's own example shows the truth: `"tags": [["p", "<recipient_pubkey>"]]` (line 229), and the top callout says "The gift wrap carries the recipient's public key in a plaintext `p` tag". NIP-17 spec confirms the p tag is the receiver. A user who follows step 4 literally will see the real recipient key and conclude their client's NIP-17 is broken.
- Fix: Replace bullet 4 with: "The event's `pubkey` should be a random one-time key (not yours); the `p` tag will still show the recipient — that is expected and required for delivery." This matches the callout, the example JSON below it, and the FAQ fix.
**54. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:73] Recommended file-sharing service nostrfiles.com is a dead domain (DNS does not resolve).**

- Evidence: "**Nostr Files** - **URL:** [nostrfiles.com](https://nostrfiles.com) - **What it does:** General file hosting for Nostr users" (File Sharing section, lines 72-75). Verified 2026-09-01: fetch fails with getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND nostrfiles.com.
- Fix: Delete the whole File Sharing subsection, or replace the entry with a verified-live option: nostrcheck.me (confirmed live, hosts media via NIP-96/Blossom with a free tier) or note that nostr.build paid plans support PDFs.
**55. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:115] Recommended stats site stats.otherstuff.org is a dead domain and appears to be an invented service.**

- Evidence: "**Stats.OtherStuff** - **URL:** [stats.otherstuff.org](https://stats.otherstuff.org) - **What it does:** Nostr network statistics and analytics" (Network Discovery & Stats, lines 114-118). Verified 2026-09-01: getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND stats.otherstuff.org. No evidence such a service ever existed under this name.
- Fix: Replace with nostr.band (the real search + network-stats service, confirmed active via third-party sources in 2026), after opening it once in a browser to confirm it loads (it refused connections from this audit environment, likely bot blocking). If it does not load, cut the entry and let nostr.watch cover relay stats.
**56. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:283] Amethyst is listed as an iOS app; it is Android-only (iOS port unreleased as of Sept 2026).**

- Evidence: "Quick reference: - **iOS:** Damus, Primal, Amethyst" (Mobile Apps, line 283). Amethyst's own repo and Google Play listing describe it as a Nostr client for Android; the iOS port is still in development. This also contradicts the site's own quickstart guide, which correctly puts Amethyst under Android only.
- Fix: Change the quick reference to match quickstart: "iOS: Damus, Primal", "Android: Amethyst, Primal", "Web: Primal, Iris".
**57. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:252] "Primal Signer (iOS)" is an invented product with invented features (FaceID-protected key storage).**

- Evidence: "**Primal Signer (iOS)** - **Built into Primal app** - **What it does:** Secure key storage with biometric authentication - **Features:** FaceID/TouchID protection, no separate app needed" (Signer Apps, lines 252-256). No product named Primal Signer exists; Primal's actual signer-adjacent feature is NIP-46 Remote Login (using Primal to sign into other web clients). The FaceID/TouchID key-protection claim is unverifiable in Primal's materials. Matches the July audit's "invented product descriptions" lead.
- Fix: Replace the entry with an accurate iOS note: iOS has no system-wide signer standard like Android's NIP-55; Primal's Remote Login (NIP-46) lets you use the Primal app to sign into Nostr web clients without pasting your nsec. Keep Amber as the Android recommendation.
**58. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:73] Recommended tool "Nostr Files" links to a dead domain (nostrfiles.com does not resolve)**

- Evidence: **URL:** [nostrfiles.com](https://nostrfiles.com) — WebFetch: `getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND nostrfiles.com`. The real Nostr file-sharing tool by this name lives at nostrfiles.dev, a different registrable domain.
- Fix: Replace the URL with https://nostrfiles.dev (verify it is indeed the same tool before swapping), or drop the entry if no longer accurate.
**59. [src/content/guides/en/nostr-tools.mdx:115] Recommended stats tool links to a dead/wrong domain (stats.otherstuff.org does not resolve)**

- Evidence: **URL:** [stats.otherstuff.org](https://stats.otherstuff.org) — WebFetch: `getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND stats.otherstuff.org`. Search confirms the live service is at stats.andotherstuff.org ("Nostr Stats", andotherstuff.org project), a different domain.
- Fix: Fix the URL to https://stats.andotherstuff.org.
**60. [src/content/guides/en/outbox-model.mdx:185] Passive relay-to-relay propagation is taught as fact, contradicting NIP-65 and the guide's own premise**

- Evidence: Line 185 (Common Misconceptions): "**Wrong:** Being on 20+ relays doesn't help. Your posts propagate through the network. Stick to 2-4 quality relays." Line 158 (Best Practices > Keep Your List Small): "- Most content propagates to popular relays anyway". NIP-65 describes only client-driven broadcasting (clients send events to the author's write relays and tagged users' read relays; clients "SHOULD spread an author's kind:10002 event" to indexer relays) and says nothing about relays copying events to each other. The guide's own opening (lines 19-26) teaches the opposite: a post to Relay A never reaches a friend on Relay B, which is why outbox exists. The site's protocol-comparison guide correctly attributes server-to-server sharing to ActivityPub federation, not Nostr.
- Fix: Rewrite line 158 to: "Followers' clients read your listed relays directly, so extra relays add nothing". Rewrite line 185 to: "**Wrong:** Being on 20+ relays doesn't help. Relays don't copy posts to each other; the outbox model sends your followers' clients straight to the relays on your list. Stick to 2-4 quality relays." Also fix the same claim rendered by the embedded quiz: src/i18n/locales/en.json, guides.outboxModel.quiz.questions[id=optimal-relay-count].explanation ends with "as content propagates naturally" - replace with "because followers' clients already read your listed relays" (and update the other locales' copies).
**61. [src/content/guides/en/outbox-model.mdx:193] Claims NIP-17 private DMs are delivered via the outbox model; NIP-17 actually uses a separate kind:10050 DM relay list**

- Evidence: Line 191-193: "### \"Private DMs go through the outbox too\" ... **Partially true:** NIP-17 (private messages) uses the outbox model for delivery, but with extra encryption." NIP-17 spec: "Kind 10050 indicates the user's preferred relays to receive DMs" and "Clients MUST only publish events to the relays listed in the recipient's kind 10050 event." DMs never route via the kind:10002 read relays this guide teaches, so a reader troubleshooting missing DMs by tuning their kind:10002 list is misled.
- Fix: Replace the answer with: "**Not quite:** Private messages (NIP-17) use a separate, dedicated 'DM relay list' (kind:10050), not the kind:10002 list from this guide - and they're end-to-end encrypted. Your client manages both lists; if DMs aren't arriving, it's the DM relay list that matters."
**62. [src/content/guides/en/outbox-model.mdx:185] Outbox guide still teaches the 'posts propagate through the network' myth that relay-guide and protocol-comparison explicitly refute.**

- Evidence: "**Wrong:** Being on 20+ relays doesn't help. Your posts propagate through the network. Stick to 2-4 quality relays." (Common Misconceptions, line 185) and "Most content propagates to popular relays anyway" (line 158). relay-guide.mdx: "Each post office keeps its own copy—they don't forward mail to each other" / "by default they don't pass it on to other relays"; protocol-comparison.mdx: "**No Federation:** Relays don't talk to each other; clients aggregate from multiple sources". This is the residue of the July 'relays share posts with each other' cluster.
- Fix: Delete both propagation claims and justify the small-relay-list advice with the guide's actual mechanism: you don't need many relays because followers' clients read your kind:10002 list and fetch from your write relays directly. E.g. "Being on 20+ relays doesn't help: relays don't copy posts to each other, and thanks to your relay list followers already know exactly where to look."
**63. [src/content/guides/en/privacy-security.mdx:196] The 'Setting Up Amber' walkthrough sends beginners to the Play Store, where Amber does not exist, and invents an Android settings step**

- Evidence: "1. **Download** from Play Store" (line 196) and "3. **Set as default signer** in Android settings" (line 198). Amber's README (github.com/greenart7c3/Amber) lists exactly four channels: Zap Store, Obtainium, GitHub releases, F-Droid; Google Play is not one of them (confirmed 2026-09-01, current releases 6.5.x). There is also no 'default signer' page in Android settings; the client opens Amber via an intent and Android may ask once which app handles it.
- Fix: Replace step 1 with the real channels (zap.store, F-Droid, or GitHub releases via Obtainium) and add one sentence warning that Amber is NOT on Google Play, so anything found there under that name is not it. Replace step 3 with: in your Nostr client pick 'Login with Amber' (or 'external signer') and approve the request when Amber opens. Safety angle: a beginner searching Play Store for a key-holding app and installing a lookalike would hand over their nsec.
**64. [src/content/guides/en/privacy-security.mdx:392] Recommending yearly key rotation as routine hygiene is wrong for Nostr and contradicts the guide's own Key Rotation section**

- Evidence: "- [ ] Regular key rotation (yearly)" (Enhanced Security checklist, line 392) and "- Periodic security practice" under 'Do rotate' (line 238). Nostr has no protocol-level key rotation; the keypair IS the identity, and the guide itself says three lines later: "**Don't rotate casually:** Lose all history, Confuse followers, Break NIP-05 connections" (lines 240-244). A creator following the checklist would burn their audience and history every year.
- Fix: Delete 'Regular key rotation (yearly)' from the checklist and 'Periodic security practice' from the 'Do rotate' list. State plainly that on Nostr rotation is a last resort for suspected compromise, accidental nsec exposure, or leaving a custodial service, never calendar-driven, precisely because identity cannot be migrated losslessly.
**65. [src/content/guides/en/privacy-security.mdx:392] Privacy guide prescribes yearly 'key rotation' as routine hygiene while quickstart states a Nostr key cannot be rotated, and the guide itself warns rotation loses everything.**

- Evidence: "- [ ] Regular key rotation (yearly)" (Enhanced Security checklist, line 392) and "**Do rotate:** ... - Periodic security practice" (line ~236). quickstart.mdx: "you cannot rotate a Nostr key or revoke access". The same privacy-security section says "**Don't rotate casually:** Lose all history, Confuse followers, Break NIP-05 connections". For the creator audience, yearly rotation means abandoning your audience every year; the protocol has no migration mechanism.
- Fix: Remove "Periodic security practice" from the 'Do rotate' list and "Regular key rotation (yearly)" from the checklist; keep rotation strictly as the compromise/exposure response ('rotation' here = starting a new identity and announcing it, which the How to Rotate steps already describe). Add one clarifying sentence that Nostr has no in-protocol rotation, matching quickstart's wording.
**66. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:25] Current Scale row is outdated and partly invented: Bluesky is ~44M, not ~25M, and Nostr's "~5M users" has no defensible source**

- Evidence: "| **Current Scale** | ~5M users | ~15M users | ~25M users |" (Executive Summary table, line 25). Verified Sept 2026: Bluesky had 43.5M registered users as of April 2026 (hit 25M back in Dec 2024). Nostr has no metric supporting "~5M users": pubkey counts are bot-inflated into the tens of millions while measured active users are in the tens of thousands.
- Fix: Change the row to: Nostr "No reliable count (tens of thousands of daily actives; key counts are inflated)", ActivityPub "~15M registered, ~1M monthly active", Bluesky "~44M registered (2026)". Alternatively drop the row and add one prose sentence with the same caveats. Update the seoTitle-implied 2026 freshness by fixing this before the next deploy.
**67. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:422] Cross-posting and Twitter-bridge recommendations are dead or fabricated: NostrPad, nostr2twitter.com, the Mastodon-Twitter Crossposter, @TwitterBridge, and "Bluesky's native tools"**

- Evidence: Line 422: "Use services like [Nostr2Twitter](https://nostr2twitter.com), [Mastodon-Twitter Crossposter](https://crossposter.masto.donte.com.br/), or [Bluesky's native tools](https://bsky.app)". Verified: nostr2twitter.com does not resolve (DNS ENOTFOUND); crossposter.masto.donte.com.br returns HTTP 503 (service died with the 2023 Twitter API shutdown); Bluesky has no native cross-posting tools. Line 397: "Use tools like NostrPad" — no such tool exists (web search finds nothing). Line 406: "Follow Twitter bridges: @TwitterBridge and similar services" and line 396 "Some accounts mirror Twitter to Nostr" — Twitter-mirror bridges died with the 2023 API closure; @TwitterBridge is invented.
- Fix: Rewrite the Cross-Posting Strategies bullet with tools verified live Sept 2026: nostrX (Chrome extension, X→Nostr), Xstr (xtonostr.com, two-way X↔Nostr), connect.nos.social (Nostr→X), and a multi-protocol app like Openvibe (formerly Nootti) that posts to Nostr, Mastodon and Bluesky at once. Delete the two "Follow Twitter bridges" steps (lines 396 and 406) entirely; there is nothing current to replace them with. Same links exist in all six translations — fix those too.
**68. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:285] Bluesky privacy block contains three false claims: phone verification required, real-name policy, and protocol-level privacy controls**

- Evidence: Lines 285-288: "⚠️ Phone verification increasingly required" (false: Bluesky requires email verification; phone is only used by the optional contact-import feature), "⚠️ Real-name policy encouraged (domain handles)" (Bluesky has no real-name policy; domain handles verify domain control, not legal names), "✅ Privacy controls in the protocol" (AT Protocol content is entirely public; DMs run through Bluesky's centralized off-protocol chat service and are not end-to-end encrypted).
- Fix: Replace the three bullets with: "⚠️ Email verification required at signup", "⚠️ All posts are public; there are no private accounts in the protocol", "⚠️ DMs go through Bluesky's central chat service and are not end-to-end encrypted". Keep the existing "Bluesky PBC has access to data" bullet.
**69. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:489] Bluesky "Future Outlook" lists as upcoming things that shipped in 2024: PDS self-hosting, open federation, and Fediverse bridges**

- Evidence: Lines 489-490: "**Self-Hosting:** Personal Data Store (PDS) self-hosting launch" and "**Federation:** Opening relay network to third parties" — both launched February 22, 2024 (Bluesky's own "Early Access Federation for Self-Hosters" announcement). Line 499: "**Bluesky ↔ Others:** Bluesky is currently isolated but planning bridges" — Bridgy Fed has bridged Bluesky and the Fediverse in public beta since June 2024 (follow @ap.brid.gy, opt-in).
- Fix: Move self-hosting and federation into the AT Protocol architecture section as shipped facts ("self-hosted PDSes have been possible since early 2024, though most accounts still sit on Bluesky-hosted PDSes"). Replace line 499 with: "Bluesky ↔ ActivityPub: live via the opt-in Bridgy Fed bridge (follow @ap.brid.gy on Bluesky)". Rewrite the Bluesky roadmap bullets around actual 2026 open questions: relay operation beyond Bluesky PBC, moderation at scale, and business-model sustainability.

**71. [src/content/guides/en/protocol-comparison.mdx:422] Recommended crossposting tool "Nostr2Twitter" links to a dead domain**

**72. [src/content/guides/en/quickstart.mdx:183] The recommended Safari signer Nostore is dead: its App Store link returns 404 and the project is abandoned, so Safari/iOS users following the guide's core safety instruction hit a wall.**

- Evidence: "[Nostore](https://apps.apple.com/app/nostore/id1666553677) on Safari" (You're Ready! section, line 183). Verified 2026-09-01 by two methods: WebFetch returns HTTP 404 and curl returns 404 on the same URL. Web search confirms the developer announced Nostore is no longer supported and the repo is archived; the app has dropped off the App Store. The maintained community fork is Nostash (tyiu), live at https://apps.apple.com/us/app/nostash/id6744309333 (curl 200).

**73. [src/content/guides/en/relay-guide.mdx:45] The guide teaches that follower relay lists must overlap with yours, contradicting NIP-65 and the site's own outbox-model guide, and never mentions relay lists or read/write relays at all.**

- Evidence: Line 45: "**Followers** receive it from relays they're connected to, so your relay lists need to overlap". NIP-65 (fetched from nostr-protocol/nips master) says clients fetch a user's posts from that user's WRITE relays, so no overlap is required with outbox-capable clients (Amethyst, Damus, most 2026 clients). The same repo ships src/content/guides/en/outbox-model.mdx ("Understanding the Outbox Model") and relays-demystified.mdx line 159 ("NIP-65 introduces a smarter way to handle relays called the 'outbox model'"), both of which this claim contradicts. The words "outbox", "NIP-65", "read relay", "write relay" and "relay list" appear nowhere in relay-guide.mdx, even though it is the category:advanced relay management guide.
- Fix: Rewrite the line 45 bullet to: followers' clients find your posts on the relays you publish to, because you advertise those relays in your public relay list (NIP-65); with older clients that ignore relay lists, overlapping relays still helps. Then add a short subsection (3-5 sentences) inside "Choosing Relays" explaining read vs write relays and that your client publishes your relay list automatically when you save relay settings, with a link to [the outbox model guide](/guides/outbox-model). No new prerequisite needed.
**74. [src/content/guides/en/relays-demystified.mdx:125] Two of three 'Interest-Based Discovery' relays are dead domains with no DNS records, including the Art relay aimed at this site's creator audience.**

- Evidence: "- **Development**: `wss://relay.nostrdevs.com`\n- **Art**: `wss://relay.nostrich.art`" (lines 125-126). Verified 2026-09-01: dig returns no A records for either host; curl gets exit 000. (nostr.bitcoiner.social on line 124 is alive, HTTP 200.)
- Fix: Remove relay.nostrdevs.com and relay.nostrich.art. Replace with community relays verified live at edit time via an NIP-11 fetch (curl -H 'Accept: application/nostr+json' https://<host>); given the creator audience, drop the 'Development' bullet entirely rather than replacing it, and keep one art/creator relay only if a live one can be confirmed.
**75. [src/content/guides/en/relays-demystified.mdx:117] nostr.wine is listed as a beginner 'Default Recommendation' but is a paid relay, so beginners who add it cannot post there.**

- Evidence: "| `wss://nostr.wine`         | General relay    | ⭐⭐⭐⭐    |" under "If you're just starting, stick with these popular, reliable relays" (lines 112-117). nostr.wine's own tagline is "The most reliable paid relay on nostr" with a one-time 18,888 sat access fee; writes from non-payers are rejected. This directly contradicts the guide's own tip on line 146: "Start with free relays."
- Fix: Remove nostr.wine from the Default Recommendations table (or relabel it 'Paid relay' and move it to the Paid Relays part of Free vs Paid). Replace it in the table with a verified-live free relay such as wss://nos.lol or wss://relay.primal.net, after confirming liveness with an NIP-11 fetch.
**76. [src/content/guides/en/relays-demystified.mdx:117] Starter relay table recommends a paid relay without saying so and two relays whose hostnames do not exist.**

- Evidence: Default Recommendations table lists "`wss://nostr.wine` | General relay" (line 117) with no paid note — relay-guide.mdx labels the same relay "`wss://nostr.wine` (paid)", and nostr.wine charges an 18,888-sat admission fee (verified 2026-09-01). Interest-Based Discovery then recommends "**Development**: `wss://relay.nostrdevs.com`" and "**Art**: `wss://relay.nostrich.art`" (lines 125-126) — both hostnames return no DNS records (checked against two resolvers 2026-09-01), so a beginner adding them gets a permanent connection error.
- Fix: In the table, either mark nostr.wine "(paid — admission fee)" or swap it for `wss://nos.lol`, matching relay-guide's free starter set. Replace the two dead interest relays with entries from src/data/topical-relays.ts and/or a link to /relay-feed-browser, which is the discovery flow finding-community.mdx already teaches — keeping both guides pointing at the same, verified list.
**77. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:77] Claims signed posts can never be re-broadcast to new relays, which is false and denies the reader the actual fix for the problem the section describes**

- Evidence: "- **Note**: Once posted, can't re-broadcast to new relays retroactively" — under 'Problem: "My Posts Aren't Showing Up"', '3. Content not broadcasted', line 77. Nostr events are signed, portable data (NIP-01); anyone can republish an existing event to any relay at any time, and mainstream clients expose this (Amethyst's note menu has 'Broadcast', Coracle and noStrudel have equivalent republish options).
- Fix: Replace the Note with: "**Good news**: your posts are signed and portable — many clients have a 'Broadcast' option on a note's menu that re-sends it to your current relays, so you can push old posts to newly added relays."
**78. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:334] Recommends Alby as a custodial wallet, but Alby shut down its custodial shared wallet on 2025-01-04 and is now self-custodial only (Alby Hub), contradicting the site's own zaps guide**

- Evidence: "- **Alternative**: Use custodial wallet like Alby" — under 'Problem: "Zaps Not Working"', '3. Wallet offline', line 334. Alby's own guides page ('Embrace Alby Hub — phasing out Alby's shared wallet') confirms the custodial service ended January 4, 2025. The site's zaps-and-lightning.mdx (line 177) already says: "Alby no longer offers a hosted custodial balance". A self-custodial Alby Hub also needs to be online, so the advice fails on its own terms.
- Fix: Change to: "**Alternative**: Use a hosted wallet like Coinos, or your client's built-in wallet (like Primal's) — these stay online for you." This matches the recommendations in /guides/zaps-and-lightning.
**79. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:224] The 'Try a Different Client' table recommends Nostros (repo archived, dead since Feb 2025) and Nos (website 404, app unmaintained since Feb 2025), and offers 'Primal web' where Primal has had a native Android app for years**

Verified 2026-09-01: GitHub API shows KoalaSat/nostros archived: true, last push 2025-02-02; nos.social root and /about both return HTTP 404 and the Nos App Store entry's last release is 2025-02-18; primal.net ships a native Android app. Nostur (App Store release 2026-08-27), Iris, Snort, Coracle all verified alive.
- Fix: Update the table: iOS (Damus) → "Nostur, Primal"; Android (Amethyst) → "Primal, Yakihonne" (both verified live); keep Web (Primal) → "Iris, Snort, Coracle" (all verified live). Drop Nos and Nostros.
**80. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:381] TroubleshootingWizard is used without an import and without client:idle, so the 'Quick Decision Tree' renders as inert HTML whose buttons do nothing**

- Evidence: "<TroubleshootingWizard />" — line 381, under 'Quick Decision Tree'. The MDX file imports only TroubleshootingQuiz (line 13). The component resolves via the components map in src/pages/[...lang]/guides/[slug].astro (line 308) but gets no hydration: the built page dist/guides/troubleshooting/index.html contains 12 astro-islands and TroubleshootingWizard is not among them, while the wizard's static markup (first question with buttons) is present. The component is a useState-driven React wizard (TroubleshootingWizard.tsx lines 361-364), so without hydration clicks are dead. Also violates the repo hard rule that interactive components use client:idle. Same bug exists in the de/es/zh/ar/hi troubleshooting files (line ~380).
- Fix: Add `import { TroubleshootingWizard } from "@components/interactive/TroubleshootingWizard";` next to the existing import and change line 381 to `<TroubleshootingWizard client:idle />`, mirroring en/relays-demystified.mdx (lines 14 and 234). Fix the other five locales' troubleshooting.mdx the same way.
**81. [src/content/guides/en/troubleshooting.mdx:334] Troubleshooting recommends 'custodial wallet like Alby', a product Alby shut down in January 2025, contradicting the zaps guide.**

- Evidence: "**Alternative**: Use custodial wallet like Alby" ('Zaps Not Working' → 'Wallet offline', line 334). zaps-and-lightning.mdx (updated 2026): "Alby Hub is a self-custodial Lightning node; Alby no longer offers a hosted custodial balance". Verified 2026-09-01: Alby ended its custodial shared wallet on 2025-01-04 (guides.getalby.com, 'Embrace Alby Hub — phasing out Alby's shared wallet').
- Fix: Replace with the zaps guide's current easy options: "Alternative: use a hosted web wallet like Coinos, or your client's built-in wallet (e.g. Primal)", and link to /guides/zaps-and-lightning#wallet-setup.
**82. [src/content/guides/en/zaps-and-lightning.mdx:334] The zap-splits setup teaches a mechanism that does not exist: splits are per-note zap tags with pubkeys and weights (NIP-57 Appendix G), not a persistent profile setting fed with Lightning addresses.**

- Evidence: "2. **Add collaborators**: Enter their Lightning addresses\n3. **Set percentages**: Must total 100%\n4. **Save**: Future zaps auto-split" (Setting Up Splits, lines 334-337). NIP-57 Appendix G: zap splits are `zap` tags on the event containing a pubkey, relay URL, and optional weight; without weights clients divide equally; the tags live on each note, so splits are configured when composing a post, and recipients are Nostr profiles, not Lightning addresses.
- Fix: Rewrite steps 2-4: splits are set per post, in the compose screen of clients that support them (e.g. Amethyst); you add collaborators by their Nostr profile (npub), the client resolves each person's Lightning address from their profile; weights/percentages are optional and default to an even split; there is no saved setting that splits future zaps automatically. Also soften step 3: percentages need not total anything, weights are relative.
**83. [src/content/guides/en/zaps-and-lightning.mdx:25] Sat-to-dollar conversions are stale by roughly 2.6x: they assume ~$30k BTC while BTC trades near $78k (Sept 1, 2026).**

- Fix: Make the conversions price-resistant instead of re-pinning them: line 25 → "(1000 sats is well under a dollar)"; line 47 → "Developer receives 100,000 sats (tens of dollars) for fixing a bug". While editing line 47, consider leading the Real Examples list with a creator example (artist/writer) rather than a developer, per the site's audience.
